   - **Plan F**: Creating two new figures (variance decomposition + type-mechanism heatmap) and integrating them into the paper
   - **Plan A Phase 1**: Spawning workers to collect public retail/airline policy documents for a prevalence audit
   - **Plan G**: Spawning and integrating worker results for per-model × per-type disambiguation awareness breakdown
   - **Convergence Analysis**: Spawning worker for attractor pattern, type×convergence, and non-convergent case analysis
   - **Plan D**: Directly writing and integrating generic vs type-specific mitigation transparency appendix section
   - **R23 Review Fixes (URGENT, in progress)**: 2 critical + 6 warning + 7 minor items. Partially completed when interrupted.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - EMNLP 2026 ARR submission with strict 8-page main text limit
   - τ²-bench agent safety benchmark with matched-pair causal design
   - Six-type ambiguity taxonomy (scopal, lexical, coreferential, incompleteness, authorization_scope, conditional_precedence)
   - Variance decomposition: clause wording 22.4%, condition 15.8%, model 1.0%, type 0.4%, residual 60.8% (~15% stochastic + ~46% systematic, OpenAI-family estimate)
   - Type→Mechanism confusion matrix (N=614 traces): structural misparse, information gap-filling, boundary violation
   - Disambiguation awareness two-cluster pattern: {GPT-4.1, Claude} 10-11% vs {Qwen3, DeepSeek} 0% (Fisher p=2.2×10⁻⁹)
   - Generic vs type-specific mitigation: generic prompt ineffective (+1.3pp overall), type-specific incompleteness has +14.0pp adverse direction
   - SESOI ±15pp with Lakens (2017) principled criteria
   - Cross-judge validation: Fleiss' κ=0.717, three-family ρ=0.71-0.89
   - Authorization scope measurement instability: κ=0.385, near-zero ICC, drives ρ=0.09 and C4 interaction

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`artifacts/review_issues.yaml`**
     - Central tracking file for all review issues across rounds
     - Updated all 11 R22 issues (W1-W6, M1-M5) from "open" to "resolved" with detailed resolution notes
     - Externally updated to R23 (15 open issues, 2 critical + 6 warning + 7 minor) — then updated to "in_progress"

   - **`docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex`**
     - Most heavily edited file. Current state after R23 N1(a)(b)(c):
     - §5.1 C1 paragraph compressed: perturbation control → short §4 reference, permutation test compressed to 1 sentence, omnibus test removed (kept in §5.2)
     - New compressed paragraph (line 8):
       ```latex
       Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), model 1.0\%, condition 15.8\%, residual 60.8\% (of which $\sim$15\% is stochastic and $\sim$46\% systematic, estimated from an OpenAI-family ICC replication; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms the clause effect exceeds chance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance. The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition; controlling for token count changes no type's predicted effect by more than 0.1\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). The confound-free range is $+$18.7\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\,pp (authorization scope); excluding incompleteness entirely, the pooled effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).
       ```
     - Plan F additions still present: Figure 2 (variance decomposition, lines 12-17), Figure 5 references
     - Table 2 has $^\dagger$ on Incompleteness (from R22), but NOT YET ‡ on Auth Scope (R23-N2 pending)
     - §5.2 three-family validation text NOT YET rewritten (R23-W2 pending)

   - **`docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex`**
     - R23 N1 merged ¶1+¶3 into single paragraph, integrated W3/W6/M1:
       ```latex
       \paragraph{Differential nocuity and intervention limits.}
       Our results support a \emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}: the binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type-specific profiles require per-domain calibration ($\rho = 0.26$). Of the 60.8\% unexplained variance, $\sim$15\% is stochastic and $\sim$46\% systematic (OpenAI-family estimate; ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}); candidate sources include conversation trajectory complexity, task difficulty gradients, and latent policy structure beyond the clause level. Even perfect disambiguation would address at most the 22.4\% clause-wording component. Prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (effective for authorization scope and lexical but not incompleteness; Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Real-world policies may contain correlated ambiguities across clauses, domain-specific jargon that interacts with ambiguity interpretation, and implicit organizational context not captured by template-based pairs---factors that could amplify or attenuate the effects observed here.

       \paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}
       Type membership explains only 0.4\% of violation variance versus 22.4\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \emph{mechanism} an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (moderate association: NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\ $0.01$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
       ```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Differential nocuity and intervention limits.}\nOur results support a \\emph{differential nocuity} hypothesis \\citep{berry_kamsties_2004,willis_nocuous_2008}: the binary effect is domain-robust ($p = 0.187$) but type-specific profiles require per-domain calibration ($\\rho = 0.26$). Of the 60.8\\% unexplained variance, $\\sim$15\\% is stochastic and $\\sim$46\\% systematic (OpenAI-family estimate; ICC $= 0.749$; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}); candidate sources include conversation trajectory complexity, task difficulty gradients, and latent policy structure beyond the clause level. Even perfect disambiguation would address at most the 22.4\\% clause-wording component. Prompt-based mitigation is type-dependent (effective for authorization scope and lexical but not incompleteness; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes. Real-world policies may contain correlated ambiguities across clauses, domain-specific jargon that interacts with ambiguity interpretation, and implicit organizational context not captured by template-based pairs---factors that could amplify or attenuate the effects observed here.\n\n\\paragraph{Taxonomy as diagnostic tool.}\nType membership explains only 0.4\\% of violation variance versus 22.4\\% for clause wording; however, it predicts failure \\emph{mechanism} an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (moderate association: NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), validating the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex"
  },
  "type": "attachment",
```

```json
{

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary claims; C4 is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, conditional OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). A Type III sum-of-squares decomposition\\footnote{Type III SS avoids ordering dependence in our unbalanced design; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).} partitions variance as: clause identity 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), model 1.0\\%, condition 15.8\\%, residual 60.8\\% (of which $\\sim$15\\% is stochastic and $\\sim$46\\% systematic, estimated from an OpenAI-family ICC replication; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms the clause effect exceeds chance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). Individual clause wording---not type membership---drives clause-level variance. The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound that includes information addition; controlling for token count changes no type's predicted effect by more than 0.1\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). The confound-free range is $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope); excluding incompleteness entirely, the pooled effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).\n\nReplication on two additional model families confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} suggests directional consistency ($+12.6$\\,pp mean; three of five significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). For cross-benchmark replication, directional consistency across all models is the primary criterion; effect-size calibration requires grain-matched measurement (Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy's value lies in predicting failure mechanisms an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation outcomes (Type III SS). Clause wording (22.4\\%) is the largest actionable component; type membership explains only 0.4\\%. The dashed line separates actionable factors (39.2\\%) from residual variance (60.8\\%, of which $\\sim$15\\% is stochastic and $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}).}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}.}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\\%), lexical (42.8\\%), scopal (39.4\\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nSensitivity analysis ($\\pm 5$--$20$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}) and bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirm stable extremes across all margins; mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\\pm 12$\\,pp. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}).\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope. Excluding it, $\\rho = 0.90$ ($p = 0.037$); a three-family validation ($\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}) corroborates. This discrepancy reflects judge-model pairing effects: pooled rankings across all three judge families show $\\rho \\geq 0.71$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}). C2 establishes robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound, coreferential as lower bound---not a complete linear ordering.\n\nIncompleteness is highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (confound analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\\%. The two-layer grouping (C3) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction ($p = 0.017$, adjusted threshold $0.0125$) and serves as organizational convenience rather than an empirical claim.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\\,pp across models ($\\kappa = 0.385$; interpret with caution); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific; see measurement caveats in text.}\n\\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory. A model main effect is significant ($\\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \\times 10^{-6}$), with Claude (30.3\\%) least vulnerable and DeepSeek (49.7\\%) most (cross-judge validated; Fleiss' $\\kappa = 0.717$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}). The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope---the type with lowest inter-judge reliability ($\\kappa = 0.385$), near-zero within-type ICC, and the sole driver of cross-judge ranking inconsistency ($\\rho = 0.09$; \\S\\ref{sec:results})---raising the possibility that C4 reflects measurement instability rather than genuine model$\\times$type interaction (Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap}). Excluding authorization scope, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}). We therefore treat this finding as suggestive only.\n\n",
        "numLines": 71,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 71
      }
    },
```

```json
{
```

```json
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/analysis.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\\text{type}; \\text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this. Two of the six type--mechanism mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\\to$structural misparse, 82.7\\% cross-model consistency) are empirically derived.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nAll dominant modes achieve $\\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}\n\\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$).}\n\\label{fig:type_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \\emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \\emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\\% ($\\leq$11\\% per model); Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero across all 278 combined traces. Awareness clusters by model family: GPT-4.1 and Claude~Sonnet~4.6 show non-zero rates (10--11\\%) while Qwen3-235B and DeepSeek-V3 exhibit zero awareness across all 278 combined violation episodes ($p = 2.2 \\times 10^{-9}$, Fisher's exact test); GPT-5.4 falls in between at 4.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}).\n\nWe term this pattern \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---the agentic-context manifestation of what \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} call ambiguity collapse \\citep{hu_ambiguity_2025}, here producing irreversible policy violations rather than mere response inaccuracy. The absence reflects default behavior, not inability: interactive disambiguation recovers resolution when enabled \\citep{vijayvargiya_ambigswe_2026}, and a prompt-based mitigation experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.3\\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nWe adopt `nocuous' from \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, noting an inversion: where they describe \\emph{divergent} human interpretations, our agents exhibit \\emph{convergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\\% unanimous)---a phenomenon we term \\emph{systematic nocuity}: models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\\%, OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$; subject to the information-addition confound; \\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n",
        "numLines": 37,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 37
      }
    },
```

```text
23	\caption{Violation rates (\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Tier: TOST equivalence cluster at $\pm 15$\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\pm 12$\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}
24	\label{tab:main_results}
25	\footnotesize
26	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}
27	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\hskip 6pt}cr}
28	\toprule
29	Layer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\Delta$pp\,[95\% CI] & Tier & $p$ \\
30	\midrule
31	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\!10^{-37}$ \\
32	 & Auth.\ Scope & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\!10^{-22}$ \\
33	 & Cond.\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\!10^{-17}$ \\
34	\midrule
35	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\!10^{-20}$ \\
36	 & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\!10^{-21}$ \\
37	 & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\!10^{-6}$ \\
38	\midrule
39	\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Overall}} & \textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \textbf{+34.0}\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\!10^{-105}$ \\
40	\bottomrule
41	\end{tabular}
42	
43	\vspace{2pt}
44	{\footnotesize $\dagger$\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \S\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\ref{app:confound}.}
45	\end{table*}
46	
47	The six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \times 10^{-5}$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). TOST equivalence testing at a $\pm 15$ pp margin reveals a graded equivalence structure among five types---authorization scope (41.6\%), lexical (42.8\%), scopal (39.4\%), and conditional precedence (36.8\%) form a mid-tier cluster, with coreferential (31.6\%) below---while incompleteness (53.6\%) stands apart as an upper bound---conditional on the confound controls in Appendix~\ref{app:confound}---whose ranking partly reflects an information-addition confound. Table~\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.
48	
49	Sensitivity analysis ($\pm 5$--$20$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:tost}) and bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) confirm stable extremes across all margins; mid-tier cohesion emerges at $\pm 12$\,pp. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}).
50	
51	An important qualification: cross-judge-group type rankings are discordant ($\rho = 0.09$, $p = 0.87$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}), driven by authorization scope. Excluding it, $\rho = 0.90$ ($p = 0.037$); a three-family validation ($\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}) corroborates. This discrepancy reflects judge-model pairing effects: pooled rankings across all three judge families show $\rho \geq 0.71$ (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}). C2 establishes robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound, coreferential as lower bound---not a complete linear ordering.
52	
53	Incompleteness is highest in four of five models ($\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); its unambiguous variant adds policy content, so the 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound (confound analysis in Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). Coreferential provides a confound-free lower bound at 31.6\%. The two-layer grouping (C3) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni correction ($p = 0.017$, adjusted threshold $0.0125$) and serves as organizational convenience rather than an empirical claim.
54	
55	\begin{figure}[t]
56	\centering
57	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}
58	\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\S\ref{sec:results}).}
59	\label{fig:forest_plot}
60	\end{figure}
61	
62	\begin{figure}[t]
63	\centering
64	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_heatmap.pdf}
65	\caption{Violation rates (\%) by model and type under ambiguous conditions ($n = 2{,}997$). Authorization scope varies 52\,pp across models ($\kappa = 0.385$; interpret with caution); incompleteness remains uniformly high (46--62\%). C4 interaction is authorization-scope-specific; see measurement caveats in text.}
66	\label{fig:heatmap}
67	\end{figure}
68	
69	\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\times$type interaction) is exploratory. A model main effect is significant ($\chi^2 = 31.02$, $p = 3.0 \times 10^{-6}$), with Claude (30.3\%) least vulnerable and DeepSeek (49.7\%) most (cross-judge validated; Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.717$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}). The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope---the type with lowest inter-judge reliability ($\kappa = 0.385$), near-zero within-type ICC, and the sole driver of cross-judge ranking inconsistency ($\rho = 0.09$; \S\ref{sec:results})---raising the possibility that C4 reflects measurement instability rather than genuine model$\times$type interaction (Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap}). Excluding authorization scope, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$; Appendix~\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}). We therefore treat this finding as suggestive only.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01XePvz9M3HrjV6bepDjwBoA`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Platform}
5	
6	We conduct all experiments on the original release of $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.
7	
8	\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}
9	
10	For each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\times$ 2 conditions $\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).
11	
12	Clause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\tau^2$-bench policy text (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
13	
14	\subsection{Models}
15	
16	We evaluate five models spanning four providers and two architecture families: GPT-5.4 and GPT-4.1 (OpenAI, proprietary dense), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (Anthropic, proprietary dense), Qwen3-235B (Alibaba, open-source Mixture-of-Experts), and DeepSeek-V3 (DeepSeek, open-source dense). This selection covers frontier and mid-tier proprietary models alongside open-source alternatives with different training philosophies. All API calls use temperature $= 0$, top\_p $= 1$, and max\_tokens $= 4096$, and were made on May 20, 2026.
17	
18	\subsection{Evaluation}
19	
20	We use a cross-judge design to mitigate self-evaluation bias: GPT-5.4 judges Claude, GPT-4.1, and Qwen3 traces; GPT-4.1 judges GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek traces. Because our matched-pair design compares ambiguous and unambiguous conditions within the same model--judge pairing, any systematic judge bias cancels in the treatment effect ($\Delta$pp); the cross-judge asymmetry affects only absolute violation rates, not C1 or C2 (\S\ref{sec:limitations}). Judges classify each episode outcome on a four-tier severity scale: none, minor, moderate, and critical. Our primary analysis uses moderate-or-above severity as the violation threshold; minor-only deviations (6.6\% of episodes) are excluded. Including minor violations (overall rate 49.6\% vs.\ 41.0\%) produces even larger effects without changing any directional conclusion.
21	
22	\paragraph{Cross-judge calibration.} To quantify potential judge bias, we conducted a dual-judge calibration on 100 stratified episodes (60 from GPT-5.4, 40 from other models; 65\% ambiguous).\footnote{The calibration sample stratifies toward ambiguous episodes (65\%) to ensure adequate violation events for $\kappa$ estimation; binary $\kappa$ is condition-agnostic.} Both judges evaluated each episode independently. McNemar's test reveals no systematic detection bias ($p = 0.606$; GPT-4.1-only violations: 9, GPT-5.4-only: 6). Binary agreement is substantial ($\kappa = 0.653$), with weighted four-tier severity agreement reaching $\kappa_w = 0.775$. Per-type binary agreement ranges from $\kappa = 0.385$ (authorization scope) to $0.875$ (incompleteness); severity-level divergence does not affect core conclusions since our primary analyses use binary thresholds (Appendix~\ref{app:severity}).
23	
24	\paragraph{Human validation.} Two annotators (NLP background), blind to LLM labels and condition, labeled 100 stratified episodes on the four-tier scale. Inter-annotator agreement is substantial ($\kappa = 0.798$, $\kappa_w = 0.762$). Pooled human--LLM agreement ($\kappa = 0.733$, $\kappa_w = 0.807$; per-type breakdown in Appendix~\ref{app:per_type_agreement}) approaches inter-annotator levels, validating the automated pipeline \citep{zheng_llm_judge_2024}. This validation supports pooled pipeline reliability; per-type estimates ($n \approx 32$--$34$) have wide confidence intervals and should not be interpreted as type-level reliability guarantees.
25	
26	\subsection{Controls}
27	
28	\paragraph{Perturbation control.} To confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity, we run 300 episodes with non-ambiguity perturbations (synonym substitution, clause reordering). The perturbation effect is $\Delta_{\text{perturb}} = -0.67$ pp (95\% CI $[-8.0, 6.67]$, containing zero). Even under the worst-case CI bound (6.67 pp), the residual attributable to ambiguity is $\geq$27.3 pp. This control tests surface-level perturbation specificity; the information-addition confound specific to incompleteness is addressed separately via logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Reproducibility.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\times$ 2 models $\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family; cross-provider stability is supported by C1 consistency across all seven models.
31	
32	\subsection{Statistical Analysis}
33	
34	We use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\chi^2$ tests with Cram\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. No industry-standard SESOI exists for LLM agent safety evaluation. Our $\pm 15$\,pp threshold satisfies two principled criteria \citep{lakens_2017}: it exceeds measurement noise (perturbation control upper CI: 6.67\,pp) by $>$2$\times$, and a per-type difference below this margin does not warrant distinct mitigation strategies. We acknowledge this margin is statistically motivated rather than anchored in deployment incident data, which does not yet exist for LLM agent policy violations; as the field matures, production violation baselines may provide domain-specific calibration. Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports sensitivity across seven margins \citep{anvari_lakens_2021}; the margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (\S\ref{sec:results}) provides a threshold-independent complement. We apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\ref{app:multiple_comparison}); post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for C1 and C2.
35
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PCb28FzHJJasheram5yG6e`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. We present a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy, evaluated across five models. Ambiguous clauses are consistently nocuous (i.e., harmful), increasing violation rates by 34.0 percentage points under controlled synthetic conditions ($+$18.7 to $+$36.0\,pp excluding the confounded incompleteness type). Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance versus 0.4\% for type; however, type predicts failure \emph{mechanism} an order of magnitude more strongly than violation rate, establishing the taxonomy as a diagnostic tool. Error analysis reveals disambiguation neglect ($\leq$11\% awareness) and \emph{systematic nocuity}: over 40\% of clauses triggering model-convergent violations. These results, observed in customer-service policy domains under synthetic ambiguity injection, motivate clause-level policy auditing and type-targeted diagnosis for policy-governed deployments.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}). Using LLMs to code LLM failures introduces circularity risk; however, mutual information between type (exogenous) and mechanism labels ($I(\\text{type}; \\text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits, $V = 0.45$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this. Two of the six type--mechanism mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\\to$structural misparse, 82.7\\% cross-model consistency) are empirically derived.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three mechanistically distinct failure patterns that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nAll dominant modes achieve $\\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}\n\\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$).}\n\\label{fig:type_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity. We code \\emph{disambiguation awareness} versus \\emph{surface adoption} (proceeding with a single interpretation). Overall awareness is 4.6\\% ($\\leq$11\\% per model); Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero across all 278 combined traces. Awareness clusters by model family: GPT-4.1 and Claude~Sonnet~4.6 show non-zero rates (10--11\\%) while Qwen3-235B and DeepSeek-V3 exhibit zero awareness across all 278 combined violation episodes ($p = 2.2 \\times 10^{-9}$, Fisher's exact test); GPT-5.4 falls in between at 4.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}).\n\nWe term this pattern \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---the agentic-context manifestation of what \\citet{liu_ambiguity_2023} call ambiguity collapse \\citep{hu_ambiguity_2025}, here producing irreversible policy violations rather than mere response inaccuracy. The absence reflects default behavior, not inability: interactive disambiguation recovers resolution when enabled \\citep{vijayvargiya_ambigswe_2026}, and a prompt-based mitigation experiment (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.3\\,pp, n.s.), reinforcing policy-design over prompt-level fixes.\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\nWe adopt `nocuous' from \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, noting an inversion: where they describe \\emph{divergent} human interpretations, our agents exhibit \\emph{convergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\\% unanimous)---a phenomenon we term \\emph{systematic nocuity}: models converge on the same incorrect interpretation, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. Of these, 83.9\\% share the dominant failure mode, confirming convergent---not coincident---violations. Incompleteness has the highest universal rate (24\\%, OR $= 2.61$, $p = 0.019$; subject to the information-addition confound; \\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Platform}\n\nWe conduct all experiments on the original release of $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.\n\n\\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}\n\nFor each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \\emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 conditions $\\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).\n\nClause pairs are generated through a template-based pipeline and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\\tau^2$-bench policy text (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\subsection{Models}\n\nWe evaluate five models spanning four providers and two architecture families: GPT-5.4 and GPT-4.1 (OpenAI, proprietary dense), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (Anthropic, proprietary dense), Qwen3-235B (Alibaba, open-source Mixture-of-Experts), and DeepSeek-V3 (DeepSeek, open-source dense). This selection covers frontier and mid-tier proprietary models alongside open-source alternatives with different training philosophies. All API calls use temperature $= 0$, top\\_p $= 1$, and max\\_tokens $= 4096$, and were made on May 20, 2026.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\nWe use a cross-judge design to mitigate self-evaluation bias: GPT-5.4 judges Claude, GPT-4.1, and Qwen3 traces; GPT-4.1 judges GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek traces. Because our matched-pair design compares ambiguous and unambiguous conditions within the same model--judge pairing, any systematic judge bias cancels in the treatment effect ($\\Delta$pp); the cross-judge asymmetry affects only absolute violation rates, not C1 or C2 (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Judges classify each episode outcome on a four-tier severity scale: none, minor, moderate, and critical. Our primary analysis uses moderate-or-above severity as the violation threshold; minor-only deviations (6.6\\% of episodes) are excluded. Including minor violations (overall rate 49.6\\% vs.\\ 41.0\\%) produces even larger effects without changing any directional conclusion.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-judge calibration.} To quantify potential judge bias, we conducted a dual-judge calibration on 100 stratified episodes (60 from GPT-5.4, 40 from other models; 65\\% ambiguous).\\footnote{The calibration sample stratifies toward ambiguous episodes (65\\%) to ensure adequate violation events for $\\kappa$ estimation; binary $\\kappa$ is condition-agnostic.} Both judges evaluated each episode independently. McNemar's test reveals no systematic detection bias ($p = 0.606$; GPT-4.1-only violations: 9, GPT-5.4-only: 6). Binary agreement is substantial ($\\kappa = 0.653$), with weighted four-tier severity agreement reaching $\\kappa_w = 0.775$. Per-type binary agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.385$ (authorization scope) to $0.875$ (incompleteness); severity-level divergence does not affect core conclusions since our primary analyses use binary thresholds (Appendix~\\ref{app:severity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Human validation.} Two annotators (NLP background), blind to LLM labels and condition, labeled 100 stratified episodes on the four-tier scale. Inter-annotator agreement is substantial ($\\kappa = 0.798$, $\\kappa_w = 0.762$). Pooled human--LLM agreement ($\\kappa = 0.733$, $\\kappa_w = 0.807$; per-type breakdown in Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}) approaches inter-annotator levels, validating the automated pipeline \\citep{zheng_llm_judge_2024}. This validation supports pooled pipeline reliability; per-type estimates ($n \\approx 32$--$34$) have wide confidence intervals and should not be interpreted as type-level reliability guarantees.\n\n\\subsection{Controls}\n\n\\paragraph{Perturbation control.} To confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity, we run 300 episodes with non-ambiguity perturbations (synonym substitution, clause reordering). The perturbation effect is $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$ pp (95\\% CI $[-8.0, 6.67]$, containing zero). Even under the worst-case CI bound (6.67 pp), the residual attributable to ambiguity is $\\geq$27.3 pp. This control tests surface-level perturbation specificity; the information-addition confound specific to incompleteness is addressed separately via logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reproducibility.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 models $\\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family; cross-provider stability is supported by C1 consistency across all seven models.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Analysis}\n\nWe use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\\chi^2$ tests with Cram\\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \\citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. No industry-standard SESOI exists for LLM agent safety evaluation. Our $\\pm 15$\\,pp threshold satisfies two principled criteria \\citep{lakens_2017}: it exceeds measurement noise (perturbation control upper CI: 6.67\\,pp) by $>$2$\\times$, and a per-type difference below this margin does not warrant distinct mitigation strategies. We acknowledge this margin is statistically motivated rather than anchored in deployment incident data, which does not yet exist for LLM agent policy violations; as the field matures, production violation baselines may provide domain-specific calibration. Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports sensitivity across seven margins \\citep{anvari_lakens_2021}; the margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results}) provides a threshold-independent complement. We apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiple_comparison}); post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for C1 and C2.\n",
```
